The compact double category 𝕀 ​ 𝗇𝗍 ​ ( 𝖯𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⋆ ) \mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}) models control flow and data transformations


Abstract
Hasegawa showed that control flow in programming languages—while loops and if-then-else statements—can be modeled using traced cocartesian categories, such as the category 𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⋆ \mathsf{Set}_{\star} of pointed sets. In this paper we define an operad 𝒲 \mathcal{W} of wiring diagrams that provides syntax for categories whose control flow moreover includes data transformations, including deleting, duplicating, permuting, and applying pre-specified functions to variables. In the most basic version, the operad underlies 𝖨𝗇𝗍 ⁡ ( 𝖯𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⋆ ) \mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}) , where 𝖨𝗇𝗍 ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) \mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T}) denotes the free compact category on a traced category 𝒯 \mathcal{T} , as defined by Joyal, Street, and Verity; to do so, we show that 𝖯𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⋆ \mathsf{Poly}_{\star} , as well as any multivariate version of it, is traced. We show moreover that whenever 𝒯 \mathcal{T} is uniform—a condition also defined by Hasegawa and satisfied by 𝖯𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⋆ \mathsf{Poly}_{\star} —the resulting 𝖨𝗇𝗍 \mathsf{Int} -construction extends to a double category 𝕀 ​ 𝗇𝗍 ​ ( 𝒯 ) \mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{T}) , which is compact in the sense of Patterson. Finally, we define a universal property of the double category 𝕀 ​ 𝗇𝗍 ​ ( 𝖯𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⋆ ) \mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}) and 𝕀 ​ 𝗇𝗍 ​ ( 𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⋆ ) \mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathsf{Set}_{\star}) by which one can track trajectories as they move through the control flow associated to a wiring diagram.

Chapter 1
Introduction

In this paper we give a wiring diagram syntax for modeling control flow and data transformations, as found throughout computer programming. The wiring diagram syntax looks like this:

(1)

Here we see two inner boxes,
A
and
B
, and one outer box. A box is formally a pair, e.g.
A=(A^{-},A^{+})
, consisting of a left side and a right side—representing input and output respectively—each with some number of blue regions that themselves contain some number of ports. These boxes can be represented by pairs of polynomial functors, as we will explain in subsequent sections; for example
A^{-}\coloneqq{\mathcal{y}}^{2}
and
A^{+}\coloneqq{\mathcal{y}}+{\mathcal{y}}^{3}
. Connecting these boxes are blue tubes that carry the control flow—i.e. case logic, sequential computation, and while loops—and within them thin black wires that carry the data as it is passed around the diagram. The syntax is
operadic
in the sense that if
A
or
B
contains a still smaller diagram of the same sort, we could nest it inside
\Phi
to get a more detailed diagram; see (
20
) for a depiction.

This syntax is modeled categorically by a compact closed category denoted
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
, where
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
is the traced monoidal category
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}\coloneqq 1/\mathsf{Poly}
of pointed polynomial functors, and
\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})
denotes Joyal-Street-Verity’s free construction
[
Joyal.Street.Verity:1996a
]
of a compact closed category from a traced monoidal category
\mathcal{T}
. For example, the diagram in
Eq.

1
is a map in
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
of the form

\Phi\colon\big(({\mathcal{y}}^{2},{\mathcal{y}}+{\mathcal{y}}^{3})+(2{\mathcal{y}},{\mathcal{y}}+{\mathcal{y}}^{2})\big)\hbox to38pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.99997pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

In
[
hasegawa1997models
]
, Hasegawa explained how cocartesian traced monoidal categories can be understood as handling control flow.
1
1

1

According to the Böhm-Jacopini theorem
[
bohm1966flow
]
, handling control flow is sufficient for computing any computable function.

A key example is
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
, the category of sets and partial functions: given a partial function
f\colon A+U\rightharpoonup B+U
, one obtains a partial function
\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(f)\colon A\rightharpoonup B
by running a “while loop”: repeatedly running
f
and plugging any
U
-outputs back in as inputs to
f
until either we terminate with a
B
or fail to do so. Later in
[
hasegawa2004uniformity
]
, he explained a naturality condition called
uniformity
, which exists on certain traced monoidal categories, including
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
.

It turns out that uniformity is a very powerful principle for traced categories. If
\mathcal{U}
is uniform traced monoidal, then so is the category
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{U})
for any category
\mathcal{C}
: both the monoidal structure and the trace can be given pointwise. This fact will be used to show that
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
, as well as a multivariate version of it, is traced, as we stated earlier without justification. Moreover, the
\mathsf{Int}
construction described above actually fits into a double category structure,
\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})
. We will discuss background on traced monoidal categories, the one-dimensional
\mathsf{Int}
construction, uniformity, and the traced structure on
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
in
Chapter

2
.

Before moving on, we briefly touch on what we mean by
data transformations
in the title of this paper. At the most primitive level, this means deleting, duplicating, and permuting variables, e.g. the map

((x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\mapsto(x_{3},x_{2},x_{3}))\colon X\times X\times X\to X\times X\times X,

(2)

which is natural in
X
. In wiring diagrams, this is done by terminating, splitting, or swapping data wires within a control region. One can also add typing to the variables, i.e. have a set
L
and a type
X_{\ell}
for each
\ell:L
, and consider natural maps of the form
\prod_{a:A}X_{\ell(a)}\to\prod_{b:B}X_{\ell(b)}
. Even more generally, one could consider the oplax slice over some category
\mathcal{L}
:
2
2

2

In (
2
) these were
\mathcal{L}=1
,
A=B=3
,
F(`1`)=`3`,F(`2`)=`2`,F(`3`)=`3`
, and
\varphi=\mathrm{id}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xRightarrow{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

(3)

Note that a diagram such as (
3
), together with a functor
X\colon\mathcal{L}\to\mathsf{Set}
, induces a function
\lim_{a\in\mathcal{A}}X_{\ell_{A}(a)}\to\lim_{b\in\mathcal{B}}X_{\ell_{B}(b)}
. We refer to these maps as
data transformations
because they are exactly the natural transformations between conjunctive database queries, and we note that they allow applying pre-specified functions (
X
applied to maps in
\mathcal{C}
) to variables. One may always rewrite a multivariate polynomial such as
x_{1}x_{2}x_{2}+x_{3}x_{3}+1
instead using the syntax
{\mathcal{y}}^{\{1,2,2\}}+{\mathcal{y}}^{\{3,3\}}+{\mathcal{y}}^{\{\}}
. The case of nondiscrete
\mathcal{L}
allows one to write diagrams in the exponents, e.g.

{\mathcal{y}}^{2\to 1\leftarrow 2}+{\mathcal{y}}^{\{3,3\}}+{\mathcal{y}}^{\{\}},

(4)

if there is a map
2\to 1
in
\mathcal{L}
. In this case, data transformations would include maps such as
{\mathcal{y}}^{2\to 1\leftarrow 2}\to{\mathcal{y}}^{\{2,2\}}
. Whenever we discuss
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
below, we implicitly mean to include any of the above sorts of data transformations.

Chapter

3
is about using the operad
\mathcal{W}
underlying
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
as a wiring diagram syntax. After some preliminaries on wiring diagrams, we will discuss the wiring diagram syntax provided by
\mathcal{W}
and hence justify the above discussion about (
1
). We then show that algebras
F\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathsf{Set}
correspond to specifying what is allowed to “fill” each box in a wiring diagram. We will show that such functors
F
give rise to traced monoidal categories in their own right. Finally we discuss what we call
bypassing
, whereby certain data can be stored while the computation within a given box is running. This is achieved mathematically using a
\mathsf{Para}
-construction. In
we consider the example of the factorial function as a case where bypassing is useful.

Finally in
we describe the double-categorical
\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}
construction on uniform traced categories, and show that the result is compact closed in the sense of Patterson
[
patterson2024toward
]
. We will also give a simple-minded factorization system on the tight category of
\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})
. This in turn will allow us to consider trajectories—the passing of control as it moves through a wiring diagram—using a universal property called
segmentation
. We will give a conditions on
\mathcal{U}
that guarantee
\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})
is segmented, and show that both
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
and
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
satisfy those conditions.

Basic background and notation.

We assume the reader is familiar with categories, coproducts
(0,+,\sum)
and products
(1,\times,\prod)
, functors, natural transformations, monads and their Kleisli categories, symmetric monoidal categories (SMCs), lax and strong monoidal functors, string diagrams, and (colored) operads. See
[
Leinster:2014a
,
macLane1998categories
,
selinger2010survey
,
fong2019seven
]
for background.

We denote the set of maps
A\to B
in a category
\mathcal{C}
by
\mathcal{C}(A,B)
or
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
. We allow ourselves to write either
g\circ f
or
f\mathbin{\fatsemi}g
for the composite
\bullet\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$f$}}\bullet\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$g$}}\bullet
and either
A
or
\mathrm{id}_{A}
for the identity on an object
A
. We denote the initial (resp. terminal) object of a category by
0
(resp.
1
). We will denote symmetric monoidal categories by
(\mathcal{C},I,\otimes)
, where
\mathcal{C}
is a category,
I\in\ob(\mathcal{C})
is the unit object, and
(\otimes)\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is the multiplication, leaving implicit the unitor and associator; the braiding isomorphism for
c,d
is also usually implicit but we sometimes write it as
\sigma_{c,d}\colon c\otimes d\to d\otimes c
.

We denote the category of sets by
\mathsf{Set}
, and for any category
\mathcal{C}
, we write
\mathcal{C}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set}\coloneqq\mathsf{Set}^{\mathcal{C}}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{Set})
to denote the category of
\mathcal{C}
-sets, or copresheaves on
\mathcal{C}
. For any natural number
N:\mathbb{N}
, we may write
N=\{`1`,\ldots,`N`\}
to denote a standardized set with
N
-elements; e.g.
0
denotes the empty set,
1
denotes a terminal set, etc. For any
A:\mathsf{Set}
, we write
{\mathcal{y}}^{A}\coloneqq\mathsf{Set}(A,-)\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
to denote the functor
represented
by
A
; when
A=0
we obtain
{\mathcal{y}}^{0}=1
, the constant functor sending each
X\mapsto 1
.

Background on polynomial functors.

Since functors
\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
are closed under coproducts, the notation
\sum_{i:I}{\mathcal{y}}^{A_{i}}
for a functor
\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
has by now been defined; it is given by
X\mapsto\sum_{i:I}\mathsf{Set}(A_{i},X)
. Such functors (sums of representables) are called
polynomial functors
on
\mathsf{Set}
, even if
I
or any
A_{i}
is infinite; for example
{\mathcal{y}}+1
is the functor sending
X\mapsto X+1
, and
{\mathcal{y}}^{\mathbb{N}}+\mathbb{Z}
is the functor sending
X\mapsto X^{\mathbb{N}}+\mathbb{Z}
. We denote the category of polynomial functors and natural transformations by
\mathsf{Poly}
; it is the free distributive category on one object. Coproducts and products in
\mathsf{Poly}
are given by the usual polynomial sum
(0,+,\sum)
and product
(1,\times,\prod)
, e.g.
({\mathcal{y}}+1)\times({\mathcal{y}}+1)\cong{\mathcal{y}}^{2}+2{\mathcal{y}}+1
. There is a fully faithful distributive monoidal functor
\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Poly}
sending
A\mapsto\sum_{a:A}{\mathcal{y}}
, which we denote simply by
A:\mathsf{Poly}
. We sometimes write
pq\coloneqq p\times q
, e.g.
A{\mathcal{y}}^{2}=\sum_{a:A}{\mathcal{y}}^{2}\cong(\sum_{a:A}{\mathcal{y}}^{0})\times{\mathcal{y}}^{2}=A\times{\mathcal{y}}^{2}
.

The remaining paragraph of the background in
Chapter

1
will not be necessary for a first reading, unless the reader is specifically interested in more advanced data transformations. This paper should not require additional background on polynomial functors, but the interested reader can see
[
niu2024polynomial
]
for more.

For any set
L:\mathsf{Set}
, consider the category
\mathsf{Set}[{\mathcal{y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{y}}_{L}]
whose objects are polynomials in
L
-many variables and whose morphisms are natural transformations between the induced functors
\mathsf{Set}^{L}\to\mathsf{Set}
. Even more generally, for any category
\mathcal{L}
, define the category
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
to be the coproduct completion of
(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set})^{\textnormal{op}}
. As mentioned in (
4
), one can think of its objects
p
as polynomials with
\mathcal{L}
-labeled diagrams in the exponents, so when
\mathcal{L}=L
is discrete, this returns the multivariate polynomial case. There is a fully faithful coproduct-preserving functor

\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathcal{L}}\colon\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set})

sending each
A:(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set})^{\textnormal{op}}
to the functor
\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set}(A,-)
and completing under coproducts. In particular,

\mathsf{Set}\cong\mathsf{Set}[0]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathsf{Poly}\cong\mathsf{Set}[\{{\mathcal{y}}\}],

(5)

where
\{{\mathcal{y}}\}
denotes the category with one object. In both cases
\mathsf{Exp}_{L}
is as expected. Following
[
spivak2025functorial
]
, we may refer to objects in
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
as polynomials or multivariate polynomials, even though the latter is a special case.

Chapter 2
Background on traced categories

In
Section

1
, we recall the notion of traced monoidal categories; in
Section

2
we recall the free compact closed category construction
\mathsf{Int}
, in
Section

3
we recall the notion of uniformity, in
Section

4
we give some results by which new uniform traced categories can be obtained from known ones, and in
Section

5
we apply some of these to show that
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
is uniform traced.

1
Definitions and examples of traced categories

Intuitively, traced symmetric monoidal categories are monoidal categories that support wiring diagrams that look like this:

f_{1}
f_{2}

A
C
B
E
D
g

(6)

where
f_{1}\colon A\otimes D\to C
and
f_{2}\colon B\otimes C\to E\otimes D
, and where
g\colon B\otimes A\to E
is defined using a
trace structure

g=\Tr^{D}_{B\otimes A,E}((f_{1}\otimes B)\mathbin{\fatsemi}f_{2})
, as we now define. We recommend the reader consider the axioms of
Definition

1.1
in terms of their string diagram syntax, e.g. as shown in the
Wikipedia article on traced categories
.

Definition 1.1

(Traced category)
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},I,\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category. A
trace
structure on it is a family of functions

\Tr^{U}_{A,B}\colon\mathcal{T}(A\otimes U,B\otimes U)\to\mathcal{T}(A,B)

parameterized by objects
A,B,U\in\ob(\mathcal{T})
, satisfying the following axioms:

Naturality in
A,B
:

for any
f\colon A^{\prime}\to A
and
g\colon B\to B^{\prime}
, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes U,B\otimes U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A^{\prime}\otimes U,B^{\prime}\otimes U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{U}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(f\otimes U,g\otimes U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{U}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}}

(7)

Dinaturality in
U
:

for any
h\colon U\to V
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes V,B\otimes U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes V,B\otimes V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes U,B\otimes U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes V,B\otimes h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes h,B\otimes U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{V}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{U}_{A,B}}

(8)

Monoidality in
U
:

the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes I,B\otimes I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{I}_{A,B}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes U\otimes V,B\otimes U\otimes V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A\otimes U,B\otimes U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{V}_{A\otimes U,B\otimes U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{U\otimes V}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{U}_{A,B}}

(9)

Superposing:

the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\times\mathcal{T}(A\otimes U,B\otimes U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A^{\prime}\otimes A\otimes U,B^{\prime}\otimes B\otimes U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\times\mathcal{T}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A^{\prime}\otimes A,B^{\prime}\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\otimes)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\times\Tr^{U}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{U}_{A^{\prime}\otimes A,B^{\prime}\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\otimes)}

Yanking:

the equation
\Tr^{U}_{U,U}(\sigma_{U,U})=\mathrm{id}_{U}
holds, where
\sigma_{U,U}
is the braiding.

We refer to an SMC equipped with a trace structure as a
traced category
.

Remark 1.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},0,+)
be a cocartesian monoidal category. Then a trace structure on it is equivalent to an
iteration operator

\iter^{A}_{B}\colon\mathcal{C}(A,B+A)\to\mathcal{C}(A,B)

satisfying certain properties
[
hasegawa1997models
]
, such as the one depicted here:

f
f
f
=

(10)

Indeed, we can inter-define trace and iteration in terms of one another as follows:

\iter^{A}_{B}(f)\coloneqq\Tr^{A}_{A,B}(\nabla_{A}\mathbin{\fatsemi}f)\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(g)\coloneqq(A+!_{U})\mathbin{\fatsemi}\iter^{A+U}_{B}(g\mathbin{\fatsemi}(B+!_{A}+U))

for
f\colon A\to B+A
and
g\colon A+U\to B+U
, where
\nabla_{A}\colon A+A\to A
is the fold and where
!_{U}\colon 0\to U
and
!_{A}\colon 0\to A
are the unique maps.

Below are some examples of traced categories.

Example 1.3

(Cancelative monoids are discrete traced categories)
.

Let
(M,i,\cdot)
denote a commutative monoid in
\mathsf{Set}
, and let
(\mathcal{M},I,\odot)
denote the corresponding discrete symmetric monoidal category. Recall that
M
is said to be
cancellative
if
a\cdot u=b\cdot u
implies
a=b
, for all
a,b,u\in M
. For example,
(\mathbb{N},0,+)
is cancellative because if
a+u=b+u
then
a=b
. The monoid
(\{0,1\},1,\ast)
is
not
cancellative because
0\ast 0=1\ast 0
but
0\neq 1
.

The monoid
M
is cancellative iff the monoidal category
\mathcal{M}
is traced by the functions
\Tr^{U}_{A,A}(\mathrm{id}_{A\cdot U})=\mathrm{id}_{A}
.

Example 1.4
.

The category
(\mathsf{FDVect}_{k},k,\otimes)
of finite dimensional vector spaces over any field
k
is traced. Here is a simple, though basis-dependent, way of thinking about the trace structure. Let
A,B,U
be vector spaces with dimensions
a,b,u
respectively. If each is equipped with a choice of basis, then an element
M:\mathsf{FDVect}_{k}(A\otimes U,B\otimes U)
can be thought of as an
(a\times u)\times(b\times u)
matrix, or equivalently an
a\times b
block matrix of
u\times u
blocks. The trace
\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(M):\mathsf{FDVect}_{k}(A,B)
is the
a\times b
matrix given by “tracing"—summing up the diagonal elements—of each
u\times u
-block in
M
.

Before getting to
Example

1.11
, the main example of interest, we recall the notion of lextensivity and briefly discuss some related notions we will use.

Definition 1.5
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is
lextensive

[
carboni1993introduction
]
if it has coproducts
(0,+)
, it has finite limits
(1,\times_{c})
, and the following relationship holds. Suppose

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}+y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{2}}

is a commutative diagram for which the bottom row is a coproduct diagram. Then both squares are pullbacks (i.e.
x_{1}\cong x\times_{y}y_{1}
and
x_{2}\cong x\times_{y}y_{2}
) iff the top row is a coproduct diagram (i.e.
x_{1}+x_{2}\cong x
).

If
\mathcal{C}
is lextensive, a
decidably partial map

c\rightharpoonup d
is a span
c\leftarrowtail c^{\prime}\to d
, defined up to isomorphism, where
c^{\prime}\rightarrowtail c
is a coproduct inclusion. We say that
\mathcal{C}
has
all points isolated
if every map out of
1
is a coproduct inclusion.

In a lextensive category, all coproduct inclusions are monic, so decidably partial maps are in particular partial in the usual sense, and they compose by pullback in a lextensive category. In
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
, every partial map is decidably partial since every monic map is a coproduct inclusion.

The following is straightforward, so we leave its proof to the reader.

Lemma 1.6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a lextensive category. The following categories are isomorphic and have the same collection of objects:

1.

the category of decidably partial maps in
\mathcal{C}
;

2.

the Kleisli category of the monad
c\mapsto c+1
on
\mathcal{C}
.

We denote this category-up-to-isomorphism by
\mathcal{C}_{\star}
.

If
\mathcal{C}
has all points isolated then there is also a bijective-on-objects isomorphism
\mathcal{C}_{\star}\cong 1/\mathcal{C}
, where
1/\mathcal{C}
is the coslice category of objects equipped with a map
1\to c
.

By (
5
), the following proposition in particular tells us that both
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathsf{Poly}
are lextensive with all points isolated.

Proposition 1.7
.

For any category
\mathcal{L}
, the category
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
is lextensive with all points isolated.

Proof 1.8
.

This is well-known for
\mathsf{Set}
and lextensivity is inherited for any functor category into a lextensive category, e.g.
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set})
. Since the functor
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathcal{L}}\colon\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set})
creates coproducts and finite limits,
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
is lextensive. Finally,
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
has all points isolated because any natural transformation
1\to p=\sum_{i:I}\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set}(A_{i},-)
factors through some coproduct inclusion
1=\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set}(0,-)\to\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set}(A_{i},-)
, and by Yoneda’s lemma, it can be identified with a map
A_{i}\to 0
. Since
0
is a strict initial object,
A_{i}\cong 0
.

Lemma 1.9
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is lextensive then
\mathcal{C}_{\star}
has an orthogonal factorization system
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C})
, where
\mathcal{R}
(“restriction”) is the opposite of the category of coproduct inclusions in
\mathcal{C}
. If
c\rightharpoonup c^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\star}
happens to be in
\mathcal{R}
, then it has a section
c^{\prime}\to c
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof 1.10
.

By definition every decidably partial map
c\rightharpoonup d
decomposes uniquely as
c\leftarrowtail c^{\prime}\to d
where the first is a map
c\rightharpoonup c^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{R}
and the second is a map
c^{\prime}\to d
in
\mathcal{C}
. The coproduct inclusion
c^{\prime}\rightarrowtail c
associated to any
r\colon c\rightharpoonup c^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{R}
is a section of
r
in
\mathcal{C}
because the pullback of a monic along itself is itself.

Example 1.11

(Traced structure on
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
)
.

For any monad on a cocartesian category, the corresponding Kleisli category has a cocartesian monoidal structure, so
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
is cocartesian by
Lemma

1.6
. This monoidal structure is traced (
[
hasegawa1997models
]
), or equivalently by
Remark

1.2
, it has an iteration operator: given
f\colon A\rightharpoonup B+A
, our function
f^{\prime}\coloneqq\iter^{A}_{B}(f)\colon A\rightharpoonup B
can be defined by the following procedure. Given
a:A
, send it to the result of applying
f
to
a
and then repeatedly taking any output that lands in
A
and plugging it back into
f
. If this process terminates—lands in
B
—within finite time, then that is taken to be the result
f^{\prime}(a)\in B
; otherwise the partial map
f^{\prime}
is undefined at
a
.

The above procedure can be defined more precisely as follows. Using the functor
p\coloneqq{\mathcal{y}}+B
, the pointed function
f\colon A+1\to B+A+1
can be considered as a coalgebra
A+1\to p(A+1)
equipped with a map from the coalgebra
1\to p(1)
. The terminal
p
-coalgebra is
(\mathbb{N}\times B)+1
, and the induced map
1\to(\mathbb{N}\times B)+1
sends
1\mapsto 1
. Thus the following composite, which we define
\iter^{A}_{B}(f)
to be, is pointed and hence represents a map
A\to B
in
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
:

A+1\to(\mathbb{N}\times B)+1\to B+1.

2
The
\mathsf{Int}
construction

Recall that a compact category (also known as a compact closed category), is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},I,\otimes)
for which every object
X
has a dual
X^{\ast}
, in the sense that there are maps

\eta_{X}\colon I\to X\otimes X^{\ast}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\epsilon_{X}\colon X^{\ast}\otimes X\to I

such that the composite
X\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$\eta_{X}\otimes X$}}X\otimes X^{\ast}\otimes X\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$X\otimes\epsilon_{X}$}}X
is equal to
\mathrm{id}_{X}
, and the composite
X^{\ast}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$X^{\ast}\otimes\eta_{X}$}}X^{\ast}\otimes X\otimes X^{\ast}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$\epsilon_{X}\otimes X^{\ast}$}}X^{\ast}
is equal to
\mathrm{id}_{X^{\ast}}
.

Example 2.1
.

The category
\mathsf{FDVect}
is compact. Indeed, the dual
V^{\ast}
of
V
is the vector space of linear maps
V\to k
, the map
\epsilon_{V}
is given by evaluation, and
\eta_{V}
can be defined as the sum
\sum_{b:B}b\otimes b^{\ast}
over any given
V
-basis
B
and its dual.

The trace structure discussed in
Example

1.4
is the one that exists on any compact category, as we now explain.

Every compact category has a trace structure: given
f\colon A\otimes U\to B\otimes U
, define

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes U\otimes U^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes U\otimes U^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes\eta_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes U^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes\epsilon_{U}}

In
[
Joyal.Street.Verity:1996a
]
, Joyal, Street, and Verity defined traced categories and showed that the above
underlying traced category
functor has a left adjoint, which they called the
\mathsf{Int}
-construction, sending any traced category to the free compact category on it.
3
3

3

The authors of
[
Joyal.Street.Verity:1996a
]
claimed that
\mathsf{Int}\colon\mathsf{TrCat}\to\mathsf{CompCat}
was furthermore 2-functorial in the natural transformations between traced functors, but this was later proved false in
[
hasegawa2010note
]
, where it was shown that
\mathsf{Int}
is only 2-functorial with respect to natural isomorphisms.

For a traced category
(\mathcal{T},I,\otimes)
, the category
\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})
has the following objects, morphisms, monoidal structure, and duals:

\displaystyle\ob\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(A^{-},A^{+})\in\ob(\mathcal{T})\times\ob(\mathcal{T})\},

(11)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})((A^{-},A^{+}),(B^{-},B^{+}))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{T}(B^{-}\otimes A^{+},B^{+}\otimes A^{-}),

\displaystyle(A^{-},A^{+})\otimes(B^{-},B^{+})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(A^{-}\otimes B^{-},A^{+}\otimes B^{+}),

\displaystyle(A^{-},A^{+})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(A^{+},A^{-}).

Its monoidal unit is
(I,I
). We denote maps in
\mathsf{Int}
by
(A^{-},A^{+})\hbox to38pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.99997pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

is defined by:
4
4

4

Despite how it is written, the composite (
12
) is not biased toward tracing out
B^{-}
. Indeed, by the axioms of traced categories, it can equivalently be written as tracing out
B^{+}
as follows:

\Tr^{B^{+}}_{C^{-}\otimes A^{+},C^{+}\otimes A^{-}}\left(C^{-}\otimes B^{+}\otimes A^{+}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$g\otimes A^{+}$}}C^{+}\otimes B^{-}\otimes A^{+}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$C^{+}\otimes f$}}C^{+}\otimes B^{-}\otimes A^{-}\right)

or as tracing out
B^{-}+B^{+}
as follows:

\Tr^{B^{+}\otimes B^{-}}_{C^{-}\otimes A^{+},C^{+}\otimes A^{-}}\left(C^{-}\otimes B^{+}\otimes B^{-}\otimes A^{+}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$g\otimes f$}}C^{+}\otimes B^{-}\otimes B^{+}\otimes A^{-}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$C^{+}\otimes\sigma_{B^{-},B^{+}}$}}C^{+}\otimes B^{+}\otimes B^{-}\otimes A^{-}\right)

In case it wasn’t clear, given
f\colon A\otimes U\otimes A^{\prime}\to B\otimes U\otimes B^{\prime}
, we allow ourselves to write
\Tr^{U}_{A\otimes A^{\prime},B\otimes B^{\prime}}(f)
to denote what technically should be
\Tr^{U}_{A\otimes A^{\prime},B\otimes B^{\prime}}((A\otimes\sigma_{A^{\prime},U})\mathbin{\fatsemi}f\mathbin{\fatsemi}(A\otimes\sigma_{U,B^{\prime}}))
, where the
\sigma
’s are braidings.

\Tr^{B^{-}}_{C^{-}\otimes A^{+},C^{+}\otimes A^{-}}\left(C^{-}\otimes B^{-}\otimes A^{+}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$C^{-}\otimes f$}}C^{-}\otimes B^{+}\otimes A^{-}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$g\otimes A^{-}$}}C^{+}\otimes B^{-}\otimes A^{-}\right).

(12)

Example 2.2

(Integer construction)
.

Given the monoid of natural numbers
(\mathbb{N},0,+)
, one can define the group
\mathbb{Z}
of integers to have underlying set
(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N})/\sim
, where
(a^{-},a^{+})\sim(b^{-},b^{+})
iff
b^{-}+a^{+}=b^{+}+a^{-}
; this discrete category is equivalent to the essentially discrete groupoid
\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{N})
, where
\mathcal{N}
is the discrete traced category from
Example

1.3
. Moreover, the zero, addition, and negation operations in
\mathbb{Z}
are the monoidal unit, monoidal product, and duals in
\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{N})
.

Example 2.3
.

3
Strict maps and uniformity

In
[

]
, Hasegawa makes the following definition.

Definition 3.1

(Strict map, uniformity)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a traced category. A morphism
s\colon U\to V
is called
strict
if, for every pair of objects
A,B:\ob\mathcal{T}
and morphisms

and

(14)

A traced category is called
uniform
if every morphism is strict. We denote the category of uniform traced categories and traced functors by
\mathsf{UnifTrCat}
.

Unlike the string diagrams for traced monoidal categories, the string diagram representation of strict maps or uniformity is not intuitive. However, the corresponding uniformity requirement (see
[
hasegawa2004uniformity
, Thm 5.2]
,
[
goncharov2016complete
, Fig 1]
) for cocartesian traced categories

f^{\prime}\mathbin{\fatsemi}(B+s)=s\mathbin{\fatsemi}g^{\prime}\quad\textnormal{$\implies$}\quad\iter^{U}_{B}(f^{\prime})=s\mathbin{\fatsemi}\iter^{V}_{B}(g^{\prime}),

shown in string diagrams below, is more intuitive, e.g. by employing
Eq.

10
:

f^{\prime}
s
g^{\prime}
s
=
U
B
U
V
U
V
B
V

\implies

f^{\prime}
g^{\prime}
s
=
U
B
V
U
B

Lemma 3.2
.

In any traced category, each isomorphism is strict, and the monoidal product
s\otimes s^{\prime}
of strict maps
s,s^{\prime}
is again strict.

Proof 3.3
.

The first follows directly from dinaturality (
8
) and the second follows from naturality (
7
) and monoidality (
9
); see
[
hasegawa2004uniformity
, Lemmas 4.1, 4.3]
.

Not all morphisms in a traced category are strict; for example, Hasegawa in
[
hasegawa2004uniformity
]
shows that in
\mathsf{FDVect}
a morphism is strict iff it is an isomorphism.

Warning 2.1
.

The composite of strict maps need not be strict. See
[
hasegawa2004uniformity
, Proposition 4.1]
.

We next come to our main example of interest.

Proposition 3.4

(Hasegawa
[
hasegawa2004uniformity
]
)
.

The cocartesian traced category
\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
is uniform.

Proof 3.5
.

Suppose given a commutative diagram of the following form in pointed sets:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U+1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B+U+1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.6,0.6}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V+1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B+V+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B+s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(15)

Taking
p\coloneqq{\mathcal{y}}+B
as in
Example

1.11
, diagram (
15
) constitutes a map
s\colon f\to g
of
p
-coalgebras, and since
(\mathbb{N}\times B)+1
is terminal, we indeed have
\iter^{U}_{B}(f)=s\mathbin{\fatsemi}\iter^{V}_{B}(g)\colon U+1\to B+1
, as desired.

4
New uniform traced categories from known ones

Hasegawa shows in
[
hasegawa2004uniformity
]
that if
\mathcal{T}
is uniform traced, then so is its arrow category. Surprisingly, he does not show that for any category
\mathcal{C}
, the functor category
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{T})
is uniform traced, but the result is completely analogous.

Given a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{T},I,\otimes)
and a category
\mathcal{C}
, let
(I^{\mathcal{C}},\otimes^{\mathcal{C}})
be the symmetric monoidal structure defined pointwise on
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}}
:

I^{\mathcal{C}}(c)\coloneqq I\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad(F\otimes^{\mathcal{C}}G)(c)\coloneqq F(c)\otimes G(c).

Proposition 4.1
.

If
(\mathcal{T},I,\otimes)
is a uniform traced category, then so is
(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}},I^{\mathcal{C}},\otimes^{\mathcal{C}})
.

Proof 4.2
.

For
A,B,U\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{T}
, the proposed trace
\Tr^{U}_{A,B}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}}(A\otimes U,B\otimes U)\to\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
is again given pointwise, i.e. for any natural transformation
\alpha\colon A\otimes U\to B\otimes U
, the component of
\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(f)
at
c
is given by

\left(\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(\alpha)\right)_{c}\coloneqq\Tr^{U(c)}_{A(c),B(c)}(\alpha_{c})\colon A(c)\to B(c).

To see that this is natural, i.e. well-defined in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}}
, suppose given any map
s\colon c\to c^{\prime}
. The following diagram commutes because
\alpha
is natural:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(c)\otimes U(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(c)\otimes U(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(c^{\prime})\otimes U(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(c)\otimes U(c^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(c^{\prime})\otimes U(c^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(c^{\prime})\otimes U(c^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(c)\otimes U(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(s)\otimes U(c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(c^{\prime})\otimes U(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(s)\otimes U(c^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{c^{\prime}}}

so we may apply uniformity (
14
). Together with naturality (
7
), we have

\displaystyle\Tr^{U(c)}_{A(c),B(c)}(\alpha_{c})\mathbin{\fatsemi}B(s)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eqn.tr_naturality}}{=}\Tr^{U(c)}_{A(c),B(c^{\prime})}(\alpha_{c}\mathbin{\fatsemi}B(s)\otimes U(c))

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eqn.uniformity}}{=}\Tr^{U(c^{\prime})}_{A(c),B(c^{\prime})}(A(s)\otimes U(c^{\prime})\mathbin{\fatsemi}\alpha_{c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eqn.tr_naturality}}{=}A(s)\mathbin{\fatsemi}\Tr^{U(c^{\prime})}_{A(c^{\prime}),B(c^{\prime})}(\alpha_{c^{\prime}})

meaning that the required naturality square for
s
and
\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(\alpha)
commutes.

The axioms of uniform traced categories (
Definitions

1.1
and
3.1
) are straightforward, because all definitions are pointwise.

Proposition 4.3
.

For any uniform traced category
\mathcal{T}
, there is a functor
\mathcal{T}^{-}\colon\mathsf{Cat}^{\textnormal{op}}\to\mathsf{UnifTrCat}
.

Proof 4.4
.

We gave this functor on objects in
Proposition

4.1
. Given a functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, the induced functor
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{D}}\to\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}}
is strong monoidal and preserves trace because all these structures are defined pointwise.

In particular, for any
c\in\ob\mathcal{C}
, the evaluation
F\mapsto F(c)
is a traced functor
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{T}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

If
\mathcal{T}
is (uniform) traced and
F\colon\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{T}
is a fully faithful strong monoidal functor, then
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
inherits a (uniform) traced structure for which
F
is a traced functor.

Proof 4.6
.

Given objects
A,B,U\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and a map
g^{\prime}\colon A\otimes U\to B\otimes U
in
\mathcal{T}
’, we want its trace in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. So let
g\coloneqq F(g^{\prime})
be its image in
\mathcal{T}
and take the trace
f\coloneqq\Tr^{F(U)}_{F(A),F(B)}(F(g^{\prime}))\colon F(A)\to F(B)
. Because
F
is fully faithful, there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
with
F(f^{\prime})=f
, and we can take
f^{\prime}
to be the trace of
g^{\prime}
. All axioms of traced categories hold automatically.

Similarly, a map
s^{\prime}\colon A\to B
is strict in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
iff its image
F(s^{\prime})
is strict in
\mathcal{T}
. In particular, if
\mathcal{T}
is uniform then so is
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

5
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
is uniform traced

Recall
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
from
Lemmas

1.6
and
1.7
; it is cocartesian monoidal because it is the Kleisli category of a monad
p\mapsto p+1
on
\mathsf{Poly}
. In this section we establish that
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
, and its multivariate version
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
, is uniform traced.

Lemma 5.1
.

There is a fully faithful strong monoidal functor
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set}_{\star})
.

More generally, there is a fully faithful strong monoidal functor
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]_{\star}\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set}_{\star})
.

Proof 5.2
.

First note that for any
\mathcal{C}
there is an isomorphism
1/\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{Set})\cong\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},1/\mathsf{Set})
.
Suppose
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
preserves the terminal object. If it is fully faithful then so is the induced functor
1/F\colon 1/\mathcal{C}\to 1/\mathcal{D}
; thus the functor

\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]_{\star}\cong 1/\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$1/\mathsf{Exp}_{L}$}}1/\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set})\cong\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set}_{\star})

is fully faithful.

It remains to show that
1/\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]\to 1/\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set})
is strong monoidal, i.e. preserves coproducts, i.e. that
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set})
preserves pushouts of spans with the form
p\leftarrow 1\to q
. By
Proposition

1.7
, the above span has the form
p^{\prime}+1\leftarrow 1\to q^{\prime}+1
, and its pushout in
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set})
is again a polynomial, namely
(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}+1)\in\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
.

Corollary 5.3
.

The cocartesian monoidal category
(\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]_{\star},0,+)
, and in particular
(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star},0,+)
, is uniform traced.

Moreover, for each
X:\mathcal{L}\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Set}
, the functor
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]_{\star}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\star}
sending
(1\to p)\mapsto(1\to\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathcal{L}}(p)(X))
is a traced functor.

Proof 5.4
.

This follows from
Examples

1.11
,
4.1
,
4.5
, and
5.1
.

All of the upcoming results about
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
extend to the multivariate
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]_{\star}
-case, but we leave the latter case implicit for typographical simplicity.

Chapter 3
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
as syntax for control flow and data transformations

Wiring diagrams are an operadic approach to compositionality; in
Section

6
, we recall this perspective. In
Section

7
we discuss the wiring diagram syntax corresponding to the operad
\mathcal{W}
underlying
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
. In
Section

8
we explain how
\mathcal{W}
-algebras give rise to traced categories. Finally in
, we define a parameterized version of this operad to deal with programs for which some functions need to store—rather than acting on—certain variables.

6
Wiring diagrams

In this section we explain the formalism for wiring diagrams as in
Eq.

1
, reproduced in
Example

7.1
. In the operadic perspective on wiring diagrams
[
Spivak:2013b
,
Rupel.Spivak:2013a
,
Vagner.Spivak.Lerman:2015a
,
Yau:2015a
,
patterson2021wiring
,
selby2025generalised
]
, a (colored) operad
\mathcal{W}
has objects
A
that act as
boxes
and morphisms
\varphi\colon A_{1},\ldots A_{N}\to B
that act as
composition patterns
(often called
wiring diagrams
) drawn as a finite number of “small” boxes
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
inside of a “big” box
B
. An operad functor
F\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathsf{Set}
assigns to each box
A:\ob\mathcal{W}
the set
F(A):\ob\mathsf{Set}
of all “
F
-style fillers” for that box, and assigns to each wiring diagram
\varphi
the function
F(\varphi)\colon F(A_{1})\times\cdots\times F(A_{N})\to F(B)
that takes a filler for each of the small boxes and puts them together under pattern
\varphi
to make a filler for the big box.

The following example says that the composition patterns for traced categories are cobordisms; it is included for the reader’s edification and is not essential.

Example 6.1
.

The composition patterns for traced categories
\mathcal{T}
include the picture shown in (
6
): the boxes are signed sets
(A^{-},A^{+})
, or equivalently oriented
0
-manifolds, and the wiring diagrams are oriented 1-cobordisms
(A_{1}^{-},A_{1}^{+})+\cdots+(A_{N}^{-},A_{N}^{+})\to(B^{-},B^{+})
. If
\mathcal{T}
’s objects are generated under
\otimes
by a set
L
, then all the signed sets and cobordisms should be sliced over
L
. Here is a depiction of such a map with
N=2
and
L=1
:

A_{1}
A_{2}
B

\scriptstyle A^{-}_{1a}
\scriptstyle A^{-}_{1b}
\scriptstyle A^{+}_{1a}
\scriptstyle A^{-}_{2a}
\scriptstyle A^{+}_{2a}
\scriptstyle{A}^{+}_{2b}
\scriptstyle B^{-}_{a}
\scriptstyle B^{-}_{b}
\scriptstyle B^{+}_{a}
\scriptstyle B^{+}_{b}

-
\scriptstyle A^{-}_{1a}
-
\scriptstyle A^{-}_{1b}
+
\scriptstyle A^{+}_{1a}
-
\scriptstyle A^{-}_{2a}
+
\scriptstyle A^{+}_{2a}
+
\scriptstyle A^{+}_{2b}
-
\scriptstyle B^{-}_{a}
-
\scriptstyle B^{-}_{b}
+
\scriptstyle B^{+}_{a}
+
\scriptstyle B^{+}_{b}

For more general
L
, each box-port (e.g.
A^{-}_{1a}
,
A^{+}_{2b}
,
B^{+}_{a}
, etc.) would be labeled with an element of
L
and the wires would preserve these labels. We denote the operad of oriented
L
-labeled 1-cobordisms by
1\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Cob}/L
.

The data of a traced category
\mathcal{T}
with objects freely generated by
L
is equivalent to the data of a functor
T\colon 1\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Cob}/L\to\mathsf{Set}
. For any two objects, i.e. lists of generating objects,
A^{-}=(\ell^{-}_{1},\ldots,\ell^{-}_{M})
and
A^{+}=(\ell_{1}^{+},\ldots,\ell^{+}_{N})
, the set of morphisms
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}}(A^{-},A^{+})
corresponds with the set
T(A^{-},A^{+})
. For any composition pattern—any combination of compositions, tensors, and traces—applied to morphisms is determined by
T
on the corresponding morphism in
1\textnormal{-}\mathsf{Cob}
. See
[
Spivak.Schultz.Rupel:2016a
]
for a proof of this.

Something like the above idea will show up in
Section

8
.

7
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
as wiring diagram syntax

By
Corollary

5.3
we know that
(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star},0,+)
, and the multivariate version
\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{L}]
for any category
\mathcal{L}
, is a traced category (
Corollary

5.3
). Hence, we can use the definition of
\mathsf{Int}
in
Section

2
to form the compact category
(\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}),0,+)
. An object in it is a pair
P=(p^{-},p^{+})
of polynomials, its monoidal product is given by pointwise sum
P_{1}+P_{2}\coloneqq(p_{1}^{-}+p_{2}^{-},p_{1}^{+}+p_{2}^{+})
, and its morphisms are given by
(+1)
-Kleisli maps of polynomials

Warning 3.1
.

Note that
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
is not cocartesian, even though
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
is. Thus the inherited notation
(0,+)
for the monoidal structure on
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
could be misleading.

Underlying the monoidal category
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
is an operad
\mathcal{W}
with objects and morphisms given by

\ob(\mathcal{W})\coloneqq\ob(\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}))\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathcal{W}(P_{1},\cdots,P_{N};Q)\coloneqq\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})(P_{1}+\cdots+P_{N},Q)

(16)

Operad functors
\mathcal{W}\to\mathsf{Set}
are in bijection with lax monoidal functors
(\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}),0,+)\to(\mathsf{Set},1,\times)
. We may switch freely between
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})
.

.

(18)

For example, from the upper right to the lower left part of (
18
) we see the visualization of a map from the
{\mathcal{y}}^{1}
summand of
P_{2}^{+}
to the
{\mathcal{y}}^{0}
summand of
Q^{+}
. Namely, it is given by the unique element of the hom-set
\mathsf{Poly}({\mathcal{y}}^{1},{\mathcal{y}}^{0})\cong\mathsf{Set}(0,1)
.

In terms of control flow and data flow, each box has some number of blue regions which we call
control regions
. We refer to a control region on the box’s left as an
entrance
and to one on the right as an
exit
. Each control region has some number of wires, which we call
data slots
. The control is
passed
forward from one box to another, given by
\Phi
on polynomial summands, and the data slots in the latter
receive
data from the former, arising from the Yoneda lemma again,
\mathsf{Poly}({\mathcal{y}}^{A},{\mathcal{y}}^{B})\cong\mathsf{Set}(B,A)
.

So in (
17
) we would say that box
Q
has two entrances, two exits, and four control regions. The two data slots in the entrance to
P_{1}
both receive data from the single data slot in the second entrance of
Q
. And so on.

using the fact that we’re working in
\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}
. That is, in the Kleisli map
Q^{-}+P_{1}^{+}+P_{2}^{+}\to Q^{+}+P_{1}^{-}+P_{2}^{-}+1
we could have sent the
{\mathcal{y}}
component of
P_{2}^{+}
to the (implicit)
1
.

Composition in
\mathcal{W}
corresponds to nesting of wiring diagrams. That is, for any
M,N:\mathbb{N}
, objects
P_{1},\ldots P_{M}
and
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{N}
and element
1\leq n\leq N
, the function

\circ_{n}\colon\mathcal{W}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{N};R)\times\mathcal{W}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{M};Q_{n})\\
\to\mathcal{W}(Q_{1}\ldots,Q_{n-1},P_{1},\ldots,P_{M},Q_{n+1},\ldots,Q_{N};R)

(19)

takes a wiring diagram of little boxes
P_{1},\ldots,P_{M}
inside mid-sized box
Q_{n}
, as well as a wiring diagram of mid-sized boxes
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{N}
inside of large box
R
, and nests them to see the
Q
’s with
P_{1},\ldots,P_{M}
replacing
Q_{n}
, inside of large box
R
.

Q_{1}
P_{1}
P_{2}
P_{3}

(20)

While the composition formula has already been completely specified abstractly in (
16
), we spell it out in case doing so is of use.

Suppose given maps of polynomials
\psi\colon r^{-}+q_{1}^{+}+\cdots+q_{N}^{+}\to r^{+}+q_{1}^{-}+\cdots+q_{N}^{-}
and
\varphi\colon q_{n}^{-}+p_{1}^{+}+\cdots+p_{M}^{+}\to q_{n}^{+}+p_{1}^{-}+\cdots+p_{M}^{+}
. Writing
q_{\neq n}\coloneqq q_{1}+\cdots+q_{n-1}+q_{n+1}+\cdots+q_{N}
and
p\coloneqq p_{1}+\cdots+p_{M}
, the composite map from (
19
) is given by the following formula

\psi\circ_{n}\varphi\coloneqq\\
\Tr^{q_{n}^{+}}\big(r^{-}+q_{\neq n}^{+}+q_{n}^{+}+p^{+}\to r^{+}+q_{\neq n}^{-}+q_{n}^{-}+p^{+}\to r^{+}+q_{\neq n}^{-}+q_{n}^{+}+p^{-}\big).

(21)

The trace was defined for (multivariate) polynomials in
Corollary

5.3
.

8
\mathcal{W}
-algebras as structured categories

In this section, we explain how
\mathcal{W}
-algebras define structured categories. We first explain how any
C\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathsf{Set}
defines a category
\mathcal{C}
with objects
\ob(\mathcal{C})\coloneqq\ob(\mathsf{Poly})
and hom-sets

\mathcal{C}(a,b)\coloneqq C(a,b),

where
(a,b)\in\ob(\mathsf{Poly})\times\ob(\mathsf{Poly})\cong\ob(\mathcal{W})
.

To obtain the identity on
a
, we first consider the map
\iota_{a}\colon(0,0)\hbox to38pt{\vbox to7.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.99997pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Example 8.1
.

Here are the wiring diagrams corresponding to the identity
C(\iota_{a})\colon 1\to\mathcal{C}(a,a)
and the composition formula
\mathcal{C}(a,b)\times\mathcal{C}(b,c)\to\mathcal{C}(a,c)
, for the arbitrary case of
a={\mathcal{y}}+{\mathcal{y}}^{2}
,
b={\mathcal{y}}+1
and
c={\mathcal{y}}^{3}
:

\mathcal{C}(a,b)
\mathcal{C}(b,c)

We now recall a theorem saying that lax monoidal functors
\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathsf{Set}
can be identified with bijective-on-objects traced functors
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
to other traced categories
\mathcal{T}
.

Let
\int^{\mathcal{T}\in\mathsf{TrCat}}\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})\alg$denotetheGrothendieckconstructionofthefunctor$\mathsf{TrCat}\to\mathsf{Cat}$sending$\mathcal{T}$tothecategoryoflaxmonoidalfunctors$\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathsf{Set}$,i.e.\ $\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})$-algebras.Andlet$\mathsf{TrCat}^{bo}$denotethecategoryoftracedcategoriesandbijective-on-objectstracedfunctorsbetweenthem.\par\begin{theorem}There is an equivalence of categories
$$\int^{\mathcal{T}\in\mathsf{TrCat}}\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})\alg\cong\mathsf{TrCat}^{bo}.
$$
\end{theorem}\par\par\par Recallthatthefunctor\mathsf{MonCatLax}\to\mathsf{Operad}isfullyfaithful,solaxmonoidalfunctorsoutof\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})canbeidentifiedwith\mathcal{W}-algebras.\par\begin{corollary}The construction from the top of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec.w_alg_cats} extends to a functor $\mathcal{W}\alg\to\mathsf{TrCat}$.\end{corollary}\begin{proof}From \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm.schultzB} we have an equivalence of categories $\mathcal{W}\alg\cong\mathsf{TrCat}^{bo}_{\mathsf{Poly}_\star/}$andwecansimplyforgetthecoslicedata.Onecancheckfromtheproofthattheconstructionagreeswiththe$C\mapsto\mathcal{C}$constructionfromthetopofthissection.\end{proof}\par\begin{remark}Recall that promonads on $\mathcal{T}$ (that is, monoids in the category of profunctors from $\mathcal{T}$ to itself with the monoidal structure given by composition) can be identified with identity-on-objects functors out of $\mathcal{T}$.
\par The symmetric monoidal structure on $\mathcal{T}$ induces symmetric monoidal structure on the category of promonads on $\mathcal{T}$, explicitly given by the coend
$$(\mathcal{P}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{P}_{2})(a,b)\coloneqq\int^{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{T}(a,a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\times\mathcal{T}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2},b)\times\mathcal{P}_{1}(a_{1},b_{1})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(a_{2},b_{2})$$for two promonads $\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}$ on $\mathcal{T}$. Commutative monoids in promonads with respect to this monoidal structure can be identified with symmetric monoidal identity-on-objects functors out of $\mathcal{T}$.
\par Finally, one could define a \emph{complete Elgot promonad} to be a commutative promonad such that the corresponding identity-on-objects functor is traced (\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{goncharov2016complete}{}{}]}). \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm.schultzB} then identifies $\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})$-algebras in sets with complete Elgot promonads on $\mathcal{T}$.
\end{remark}\par Recallfrom\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{fong2019supplying}{}{}]}thatfor$\mathcal{C}$tohavea\emph{supply}ofcommutativemonoidsmeansthatitsobjectsarecoherentlyequippedwithcommutativemonoidstructures.\par\begin{proposition}Suppose that $\mathcal{T}$ is cocartesian traced and that $C\colon\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathsf{Set}$ is lax monoidal. Then the traced category $\mathcal{C}$ given by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor.schultz_trcat} has a supply of commutative monoids.
\end{proposition}\begin{proof}Every object in a cocartesian category is a $(0,+)$-monoid; in fact they form a supply of commutative monoids, and $C$ preserves that supply.
\end{proof}\par\par Weconcludethissectionbyofferinganexample$\mathcal{W}$-algebra.Recallfrom\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor.polystar_traced}thatforanyset$X:\mathsf{Set}$thereisatracedfunctor$-_X\colon\mathsf{Poly}_\star\to\mathsf{Set}_\star$givenbysending$p\mapsto p(X)$.Since$\mathsf{Int}$isfunctorial,weobtainastrongmonoidalfunctor$\mathsf{Int}(-_X)\colon\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_\star)\to\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Set}_\star)$.Now,foranymonoidalcategory$(\mathcal{C},I,\otimes)$,themap$\mathcal{C}(I,-)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Set}$islaxmonoidalbecause$I$isa$\otimes$-comonoid.Thusweobtainthecomposite$$\mathcal{W}=\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$\mathsf{Int}(-_{X})$}}\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Set}_{\star})\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Set}_{\star})((0,0),-)$}}\mathsf{Set}.$$Unpacking,thissendsanyobject$(p^-,p^+)\in\ob(\mathcal{W})$to$\mathsf{Set}_\star(p^-(X),p^+(X))$,thesetofpartialfunctions$p^-(X)\rightharpoonup p^+(X)$.\par Theaboveresultssaythatforany$X:\ob\mathsf{Set}$,thereisatracedmonoidalcategorywithacommutativemonoidstructureoneachobject$p:\ob\mathsf{Poly}_\star$,inwhichamorphism$p\to q$isapartialfunction$p(X)\rightharpoonup q(X)$.Onemayenforcetypingonthewiresbyusingmultivariatepolynomials;see\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor.polystar_traced}.\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Bypassing}
\par In any cartesian monoidal category, if one is given a map $f_{1}\colon B\to C$ and $f_{2}\colon A\times C\to D$, one obtains a map $A\times B\to D$. In the corresponding string diagrams, this setup would be drawn as following (without the blue):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{53.4629pt}{-6.91963pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{equation}However, it is clear that---when we do include the blue control regions---this picture does not fit within the wiring diagram syntax given by $\mathcal{W}$, since the wire marked $A$ does not stay within the blue control region. To fix this, we introduce the notion of \emph{bypass}, which in turn comes from a $\mathsf{Para}$ construction. We follow \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{capucci2024actegories}{}{}, Definition 5.1.1]} for some useful definitions. (The results in this section are technical and probably useful to anyone implementing this theory; see \eqref{eqn.w'} for the definition of $\mathcal{W}^{\prime}$. These results will not be needed elsewhere, however, so the reader may choose to skip directly to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{chap.functoriality_and_int} for the double categorical $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}$-construction.)
\par\begin{definition}A \emph{distributive algebroidal actegory} consists of monoidal categories $(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)$ and $(\mathcal{C},o,\boxplus)$, a functor $\bullet\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}$, and natural isomorphisms
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} \eta&\colon c\cong I\bullet c&\mu&\colon(m_{1}\otimes m_{2})\bullet c\cong m_{1}\bullet(m_{2}\bullet c),\\
\gamma&\colon m\bullet o\cong o&\delta&\colon m\bullet(c_{1}\boxplus c_{2})\cong(m\bullet c_{1})\boxplus(m\bullet c_{2}),\end{aligned}\end{equation}satisfying various coherence laws.
\par If $\mathcal{C}$ is traced, we say that the distributive algebroidal actegory is \emph{traced} if for all $m:\ob\mathcal{M}$ and $f\colon c\boxplus u\to d\boxplus u$ in $\mathcal{C}$, we have
\begin{equation}\Tr^{m\bullet u}_{m\bullet c,m\bullet d}(m\bullet f)=m\bullet\Tr^{u}_{c,d}(f).\end{equation}\end{definition}\par\begin{proposition}If $\mathcal{M}$ is a monoidal groupoid and $\bullet\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}$ is a traced distributive algebroidal actegory then $\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{C})$ is a distributive algebroidal $\mathcal{M}$-actegory.
\end{proposition}\par\begin{proof}The action is given pairwise: on objects $m\bullet(c^{-},c^{+})\coloneqq(m\bullet c^{-},m\bullet c^{+})$, and given morphisms $m\cong n$ and $d^{-}\otimes c^{+}\to d^{+}\otimes c^{-}$, the two obvious ways to construct $(n\bullet d^{-})\boxplus(m\bullet c^{+})\to(n\bullet d^{+})\boxplus(m\bullet c^{-})$ agree, and it is clear that we again have natural isomorphisms as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn.dist_algebroidal}. Finally, \eqref{eqn.traced_distributive} ensures that the action respects composition in $\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{C})$.
\end{proof}\par\begin{definition}Let $\bullet\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}$ be a distributive algebroidal actegory. Define $\mathsf{Para}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})$ to be the operad with object set $\ob\mathsf{Para}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})\coloneqq\ob\mathcal{C}$ and
$$\mathsf{Para}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})(c_{1},\ldots,c_{N};d)\coloneqq\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{N}:\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{C}\big((m_{1}\bullet c_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{N}\bullet c_{N}),d\big)$$In other words, a map $c_{1},\ldots,c_{N}\to d$ in $\mathsf{Para}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})$ consists of $\mathcal{M}$-objects $m_{1},\ldots,m_{N}$ and a map $(m_{1}\bullet c_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{N}\bullet c_{N})\longrightarrow d$.
\par The identity chooses the identity object $I:\ob(\mathcal{M})$ and given maps
$$(\ell_{1}\bullet b_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(\ell_{M}\bullet b_{M})\xrightarrow{\;\;\varphi\;\;}c_{n}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad(m_{1}\bullet c_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{N}\bullet c_{N})\xrightarrow{\;\;\psi\;\;}d$$we define their composite $\varphi\circ_{n}\psi$ using linearity \eqref{eqn.dist_algebroidal}:
\begin{multline*}(m_{1}\bullet c_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{n-1}\bullet c_{n-1})\boxplus((m_{n}\otimes\ell_{1})\bullet b_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus((m_{n}\otimes\ell_{M})\bullet b_{M})\\
\boxplus(m_{n+1}\bullet c_{n+1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{N}\bullet c_{N})\longrightarrow(m_{1}\bullet c_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{N}\bullet c_{N})\to d.\end{multline*}(m_{1}\bullet c_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{n-1}\bullet c_{n-1})\boxplus((m_{n}\otimes\ell_{1})\bullet b_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus((m_{n}\otimes\ell_{M})\bullet b_{M})\\
\boxplus(m_{n+1}\bullet c_{n+1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{N}\bullet c_{N})\longrightarrow(m_{1}\bullet c_{1})\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(m_{N}\bullet c_{N})\to d.\end{definition}\par\begin{proposition}The monoidal category $(\mathsf{Set}_{\star},0,+)$ is a traced distributive algebroidal actegory with respect to the monoidal category $(\mathsf{FinSet},1,\times)$.
\end{proposition}\begin{proof}Define $M\bullet C\coloneqq M\times C$ and similarly for morphisms. We have the natural isomorphisms of \eqref{eqn.dist_algebroidal} satisfying the coherences. Now given a finite set $M$ and a map $f\colon A+U\to B+U$, the map $M\bullet f$ can be constructed as the sum $f+\cdots+f$ (with $M$-many summands). By monoidality in $U$ \eqref{eqn.monoidal_in_U}, we have
$$\Tr^{M\times U}_{M\times A,M\times B}(M\times f)=M\times\Tr^{U}_{A,B}(f),$$as desired.
\end{proof}\par Let $\mathsf{FinPoly}_{\star}\subseteq\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}$ denote the full subcategory spanned by the finite polynomials $p$, i.e.\ those for which $p(X)$ is finite whenever $X:\mathsf{FinSet}$ is.
\par\begin{corollary}The monoidal category $(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star},0,+)$ is a traced distributive algebroidal actegory with respect to both the monoidal category $(\mathsf{FinPoly},1,\times)$.
\end{corollary}\begin{proof}Again, we define $m\bullet p\coloneqq m\times p$. The traced structure on $\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}$ is inherited (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor.polystar_traced}) from the fully faithful functor $\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Set}_{\star})$, so that for any $m:\mathsf{FinPoly}$, $\alpha\colon p+u\to q+u$, and $C:\mathsf{Set}$ we have
\@@amsalign
as desired.
\end{proof}\par\begin{corollary}We have distributive algebroidal actegories
$$(\times)\colon\mathsf{FinSet}^{\cong}\times\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Set}_{\star})\to\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Set}_{\star})\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad(\times)\colon\mathsf{FinPoly}^{\cong}\times\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})\to\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}).$$\end{corollary}\begin{proof}This follows from \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{prop.setstar_dta}, \lx@cref{refnum}{cor.polystar_dta}, and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{prop.int_groupoid_dta} and the fact that if $\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}$ is a distributive algebroidal actegory, then so is the induced functor $\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}$ for any monoidal subcategory $\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}$.
\end{proof}\par Thus by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def.para} we have an operad
\begin{equation}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{Para}_{\times}(\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})),\end{equation}whose objects are pairs $P=(p^{+},p^{-})$ of polynomials and whose morphisms $P_{1},\ldots,P_{N}\to Q$ are maps $m_{1}P_{1},\ldots,m_{N}P_{n}\to Q$ in $\mathcal{W}$, i.e.\ morphisms $q^{-}+m_{1}p_{1}^{+}+\cdots+m_{N}p_{N}^{+}\to q^{+}+m_{1}p_{1}^{-}+\cdots+m_{N}p_{N}^{-}$ in $\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}$ for some choice of polynomials $m_{1},\ldots,m_{N}:\mathsf{Poly}$. We refer to each $m_{i}$ as the $i$th \emph{bypass polynomial}.
\par In general, a bypass polynomial associated to a box $p$ will be of the form $\sum_{j:J}{\mathcal{y}}^{A_{j}}$. The set $J$ represents the different options $j:J$ for what data ($A_{j}$) is stored, based on the control exit from which box $p$ is entered. This choice will be available upon exit from box $p$.
\par We can now make sense of the diagram from \eqref{eqn.bypass}, where $P_{1}=({\mathcal{y}},{\mathcal{y}})$, $P_{2}=({\mathcal{y}}^{2},{\mathcal{y}})$, and $Q=({\mathcal{y}}^{2},{\mathcal{y}})$, though it is better to redraw it within the current framework: the passing wire $B$ is now held in storage (shown gray) within $P_{1}$:

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$The diagram is given by a ``para'' map $P_{1},P_{2}\to Q$ in $\mathcal{W}^{\prime}$, i.e.\ a map $({\mathcal{y}}\times P_{1}),P_{2}\to Q$ in $\mathcal{W}$. Unpacking, this is the morphism of type
$$\{Q\}{\mathcal{y}}^{2}+\{P_{1}\}{\mathcal{y}}\times{\mathcal{y}}+\{P_{2}\}{\mathcal{y}}\to\{Q\}{\mathcal{y}}+\{P_{1}\}{\mathcal{y}}\times{\mathcal{y}}+\{P_{2}\}{\mathcal{y}}^{2}$$given by isomorphisms $\{Q\}{\mathcal{y}}^{2}\mapsto\{P_{1}\}{\mathcal{y}}\times{\mathcal{y}}$, $\{P_{1}\}{\mathcal{y}}\times{\mathcal{y}}\mapsto\{P_{2}\}{\mathcal{y}}^{2}$, and $\{P_{2}\}{\mathcal{y}}\mapsto\{Q\}{\mathcal{y}}$.
\par\begin{example}The factorial function requires several instances of bypassing; it can be drawn as follows:

$$\text{One}\coloneqq(1,{\mathcal{y}}),\quad\text{If}\coloneqq({\mathcal{y}},{\mathcal{y}}+1),\quad\text{Mul}\coloneqq({\mathcal{y}}^{2},{\mathcal{y}}),\quad\text{Dec}\coloneqq({\mathcal{y}},{\mathcal{y}}),\quad\text{Fac}\coloneqq({\mathcal{y}},{\mathcal{y}}).$$The bypass polynomial for each of the internal boxes is $m_{1}=m_{2}=m_{3}=m_{4}={\mathcal{y}}$ because each box carries one variable's worth of storage. \lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\text{Fac}$ in $\mathcal{W}=\mathsf{Int}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})$.
\par The program says to read $N$ and store it while outputting $T\coloneqq 1$, to serve as the ``total''. Then store $T$ and if $N\leq 1$, output $T$. If $N>1$ then send $N$ and $T$ off to be multiplied while $N$ is stored. The resulting new total is stored while the $N$ is decremented; repeat.
\end{example}\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{chapter}{toc}{Double categorical $\IInt$ construction and applications}
\par In \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec.double_cat_Int} we show that for any uniform traced category $\mathcal{U}$ (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def.strict_uniform}), the free compact category $\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{U})$ forms the loose part of a thin double category $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$, whose tight cells are just pairs of maps in $\mathcal{U}$. We also show that $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$ is compact in the sense of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{patterson2024toward}{}{}]}. We also discuss a very simple orthogonal factorization system on the tight maps, which will be useful in the final section, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec.trajectories}, where we show an application of the double category: it lets us model control-flow \emph{trajectories} through a wiring diagram.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Double category structure on $\IInt(\cat{U})$}
\par\begin{theorem}Let $(\mathcal{U},I,\otimes,\Tr)$ be a uniform traced category. There is a thin symmetric monoidal double category $(\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U}),(I,I),(\otimes,\otimes))$, for which the category of tight maps is $\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}$, for which loose maps are given by the $\mathsf{Int}$ construction \eqref{eqn.int_construction}, and for which a cells exists iff the obvious diagram commutes. Explicitly,
\begin{description}\description@item@[Object:]a pair of objects $(A^{-},A^{+})\in\ob(\mathcal{U})^{2}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\description@item@[Monoidal:]unit is $(I,I)$ and product is $(A^{-},A^{+})\otimes(B^{-},B^{+})\coloneqq(A^{-}\otimes B^{-},A^{+}\otimes B^{+})$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\description@item@[Tight map:]a pair of maps $s^{-}\colon A_{1}^{-}\to A_{2}^{-}$ and $s^{+}\colon A_{1}^{+}\to A_{2}^{+}$ in $\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\description@item@[Loose map:]a map $f\colon B^{-}\otimes A^{+}\to B^{+}\otimes A^{-}$ in $\mathcal{U}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\description@item@[Cell:]\begin{equation}\hbox to140.95pt{\vbox to57.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.64516pt\lower-29.04968pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$commutes by a combination of naturality \eqref{eqn.tr_naturality} and uniformity \eqref{eqn.uniformity}. Checking that this double category is symmetric monoidal is straightforward, completing the proof.
\end{proof}\par\begin{theorem}The monoidal double category $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$ is compact in the sense of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{patterson2024toward}{}{}]}, for any uniform traced category $\mathcal{U}$.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}We need to provide a dual $(-)^{*}\colon\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})^{\textnormal{co}}\to\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$, where $-^{\textnormal{co}}$ means loose-opposite, and then check several axioms. Define $(A^{-},A^{+})^{*}\coloneqq(A^{+},A^{-})$; it is clearly functorial and satisfies
$$\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})(A\otimes B,C)\cong\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})(A,B^{*}\otimes C),$$since there is a bijection between maps $C^{-}\otimes A^{+}\otimes B^{+}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$f^{\sharp}$}}C^{+}\otimes A^{-}\otimes B^{-}$ and maps $B^{+}\otimes C^{-}\otimes A^{+}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{$f^{\flat}$}}B^{-}\otimes C^{+}\otimes A^{-}$.
\par It is also easy to check that for tight maps $s\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}$, $t\colon B_{1}\to B_{2}$ and $u\colon C_{1}\to C_{2}$, the left diagram below commutes iff the right one does:

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}C^{\prime}$, the axioms of $\mathsf{Int}(\mathcal{U})$ as a compact 1-category imply the remaining stated condition, that $a\mathbin{\fatsemi}f^{\flat}\mathbin{\fatsemi}(b^{*}\otimes c)\cong((a\otimes b)\mathbin{\fatsemi}f^{\sharp}\mathbin{\fatsemi}c)^{\flat}$. We leave the remaining coherences to the reader.
\end{proof}\par The following is straightforward; we record it here for use in the next section.
\par\begin{lemma}Let $\mathcal{U}$ be uniform traced monoidal, and let $\mathcal{I}^{-}$ (resp.\ $\mathcal{I}^{+}$) denote the class of those tight maps $(f^{-},f^{+})\colon(A_{1}^{-},A_{1}^{+})\to(A_{2}^{-},A_{2}^{+})$ in $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$ for which $A_{1}^{-}=A_{2}^{-}$ and $f^{-}$ is the identity (resp.\ $A_{1}^{+}=A_{2}^{+}$ and $f^{+}$ is the identity).
\par Then $(\mathcal{I}^{-},\mathcal{I}^{+})$ forms a strict factorization system on the tight maps of $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$, and so does $(\mathcal{I}^{+},\mathcal{I}^{-})$.
\end{lemma}\par\begin{remark}Recall (\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{grandis2000weak}{}{}]}) that any strict factorization system gives rise to an orthogonal factorization system by closing the left (resp.\ right) class under precomposition (resp.\ postcomposition) with isomorphisms.
\end{remark}\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Trajectories}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{equation}How can we find this within the mathematical formalism? We begin by defining a universal property we call \emph{segmentation}, which may exist on a double category. We prove \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm.segmentation} which gives sufficient conditions on $\mathcal{U}$ by which $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$ is segmented, and we use it to show that $\mathsf{Set}_{\star}$ and $\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}$ are each segmented. Finally we use segmentation to explain how to get the trajectory in \eqref{eqn.traj_wd}
\par\begin{definition}Let $\mathbb{D}$ be a double category. We say $\mathbb{D}$ is \emph{segmented} if there exist two classes $(\mathcal{I}^{-},\mathcal{I}^{+})$ of tight maps satisfying the following universal property: For any 2-cell of the form shown left in \eqref{eqn.segment_1}, there is a factorization $\phi=\phi_{1}\mathbin{\fatsemi}\phi_{2}$ having the form shown right:
\begin{equation}\hbox to87.29pt{\vbox to87.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.64598pt\lower-43.84987pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-43.64598pt}{-37.46936pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{equation}\end{definition}\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}Suppose that $\mathcal{U}$
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@is cocartesian traced monoidal and uniform,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@has pushouts,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@has an orthogonal factorization system $(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S})$, such that every map in $\mathcal{R}$ has a section in $\mathcal{U}$ and such that the category $\mathcal{S}$ is extensive.
\end{itemize}Then the double category $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$ from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm.IInt} is segmented.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}We take $(\mathcal{I}^{-},\mathcal{I}^{+})$ to be the classes of maps from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem.fact_system_IInt}.
Suppose given a diagram in $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathcal{U})$, as in the left of \eqref{eqn.segment_1}:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$There is a universal map $P\to{D^{\prime}}^{+}+{C^{\prime}}^{-}$, and since the map $P\to X$ from \eqref{eqn.factorization} is in $\mathcal{R}$ and hence has a section $X\to P$, we have the composite $B_{2}^{+}+A_{2}^{-}=X\to P\to{D^{\prime}}^{+}+{C^{\prime}}^{-}$. Moreover, this map takes place over $D^{+}+C^{-}$ and hence can be written as the sum of maps
$$B_{2}^{+}\to{D^{\prime}}^{+}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad A_{2}^{-}\to{C^{\prime}}^{-}.$$Thus we have the desired factorization:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\end{proof}\par\begin{corollary}The double categories $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathsf{Set}_{\star})$ and $\mathbb{I}\mathsf{nt}(\mathsf{Poly}_{\star})$ are each segmented.
\end{corollary}\begin{proof}It suffices to show that $\mathsf{Set}_{\star}$ and $\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}$ satisfy the properties in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm.segmentation}. Both $\mathsf{Set}_{\star}$ and $\mathsf{Poly}_{\star}$ are cocartesian traced monoidal and uniform by \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{prop.setstar_traced} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{cor.polystar_traced}. Both have pushouts because the forgetful functor from a coslice category reflects all connected colimits, and $\mathsf{Set}$ and $\mathsf{Poly}$ have pushouts. The third criterion is \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{prop.complemented_points} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{lem.ofs_cstar}.
\end{proof}\par We now show how to use segmentation from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor.star_segmentation} to obtain trajectories. The picture in \eqref{eqn.traj_wd} is the result of tracing a trajectory that begins with only the following:
